2\. Create 5 questions about retention/completion rates (use stats from project knowledge)

3\. Test on 10 ICPs via Loom this week (as outlined in your whiteboard plan)

4\. Track response rate and iterate (aim for 30%+ response rate)

5\. Build first version of webinar (Pop Quiz + Second Harvest frame)

Your Hook Evolution Path:

Month 1-2: Pop Quiz (simple, proven) Month 3-4: Add Fortune Teller (as you get case studies) Month

5-6: Manufacture "Hype But True" hook (with impressive numbers) Month 7-12: Create your "Ramp

Engine" hook (find your authority story)

The Hook That Will Make You $300K-$400K Year 1:

"The 10% Problem: Why 90% of Your Students Are Failing (And How to Fix It in 90 Days)"

Simple. Specific. Shocking. Solvable.

That's your hook.

Now go test it.

Jason Fladlien - The Power of Pain in Webinars

Complete Study Notes & Frameworks

THE CORE PHILOSOPHY: WHY PAIN MATTERS

The Fundamental Truth

People will spend more money, faster, to get OUT of pain than they will to GET pleasure.

The analogy Jason uses: Who will spend more money faster?

Joe with a desire for a brighter, whiter smile

Jane with a crippling toothache

Jane wins every time. Pain is the superior motivator.

The Success Paradox

The more successful you become, the further you get from your audience's pain. You've solved the

problem they're facing, so you no longer carry the emotional baggage around it. This is where success

becomes a curse in marketing.

Jason's admission: "I cannot relate to somebody who doesn't have money. I don't really know what that's

like. So it's really hard for me to know the struggles and pains that they face. I have to force myself to

continually try to look at it through their eyes."

The discipline required: You must constantly force yourself to see through your customer's eyes,

especially as you get more successful and further removed from their daily struggles.

THE MICHELANGELO PRINCIPLE: SUBTRACTION BEFORE

ADDITION

The Statue of David Concept

Michelangelo didn't add anything to create David - he removed everything that wasn't David.

Applied to sales: "If I just remove the excuses, they will buy. I don't have to add anything."

This is Jason's core attitude: Subtraction before addition is super effective from a strategic problem-

solving position.

The Time Objection Close Example

When a client says "I don't have time":

Jason's approach:

1\. "Come up with the five things that take up most of your time that are important to you"

2\. "List them 1 through 5"

3\. "Look at \#5 - how many hours per week do you put to that task?"

4\. "You're not getting the results you want, right?"

5\. "I'm only asking you to replace \#5 with my thing, and only do my thing HALF as long as you do \#5"

6\. "Congratulations, I've just freed up more time for you"

The result: He subtracted from their life, didn't add anything. More effective than addition.

THE COMPLETE PAIN DISCOVERY GRID

Jason shows that most questions in customer research are centered around pain. Here's the comprehensive

breakdown:

What Terrifies Them?

Where is there massive uncertainty in their life?

What could be taken from them at any minute?

What dangers do they face, real and imagined?

Where are they hanging by a thread?

What Angers Them? (Anger is a Gift)

Who do they think lies to them?

Who do they blame for their shortcomings?

Who has violated their trust in the past?

What grievous injustices have befell them?

Who would they like to punish?

What freedoms have been taken from them?

Where do they feel cheated?

Where do they feel powerless?

Jason's note: Anger is a kind of pain. He separates them but acknowledges they're connected. "Anger is a

gift" - reference to Rage Against the Machine.

What Are They Desperate For?

Desperation itself is a pain state.

Daily Obstacles They Encounter

Where are they stuck, unable to make progress?

What bores them to death?

What have they been neglecting?

Repetitive Thoughts Running in Their Head

Jason's observation: "People don't have repetitive thoughts like 'Damn I'm good, damn everything's

good, I can't stop thinking about everything's good.' No - it's bad, bad stuff."

Repetitive thoughts are ALWAYS negative:

What do they feel like a failure about?

What do they feel unworthy of?

Where do they feel incapable?

What do they think they'll always be misunderstood about?

What are their regrets?

Recent Disruptions They've Encountered

Scandals made public

News cycles

Political/social change

New thought leaders

Technological disruptions

Jason's note: "Never let a good crisis go to waste" - Rahm Emanuel

The Simplified Pain List

When you strip everything down, you're looking for:

1\. Excuses - What are their excuses?

2\. Limiting Beliefs - What are their limiting beliefs?

3\. Complaints - What are their complaints?

4\. Obstacles - What are their obstacles?

5\. Enemies - What are their enemies?

6\. Fears - What are their fears?

Jason thinks about these constantly: "My attitude is if I just remove the excuses, they will buy. I don't

have to add anything."

THE 8 PAIN STRATEGIES FOR WEBINARS

Strategy \#1: Challenges You Face

The Framework:

External - Things outside their control

Internal - Things they can control but don't

Commitment - Get them to commit to move forward

The principle: External events create internal states.

Example: You text somebody, they don't text back → Internal state: "They must hate me" Reality: They

were out of cell phone reception.

The approach: Address BOTH the external events AND the internal states they create, then get

commitment to move forward in spite of (or because of) these challenges.

Jason's ASM5 Example: "If I had to guess:

You have more opportunities placed in front of you than you can even consider, much less take

advantage of

You often let little things get in your way

You're afraid to make wrong decisions

Support from others for what you're trying to do isn't what you'd like it to be

Most marketers do not have your best interest in mind when they're trying to 'solve your problems'

You face confusion and conflicting information on a daily basis"

Jason's note: "Put this in front of any market and they'll agree to it. This is a cold read in the sense, but

it's true."

Today he'd say it more directly without flowery language, but the principle remains the same.

Strategy \#1B: Exaggerated Cynicism with a Dash of Hope

The formula:

Confrontational

Inspirational

Accountability

Viktor Frankl principle: "People will hold on to a glimmer of hope in the face of nearly insurmountable

odds."

The approach: Make their situation look dark, BUT give them just a sprinkle of hope - like salt, just kind

of sprinkle it in there. People will hold onto a glimmer of hope even when faced with nearly

insurmountable odds.

Jason's note: "You gotta give them just a glimmer of hope that maybe, possibly, their life isn't a complete

waste."

Example from ASM5: After showing testimonials, anticipate their objections: "You might be thinking

these are 1 in 10,000 customers, that they already had assets and advantages in place, you might be

thinking 'well that's their gross Jason but what's their net'..."

Then provide the dash of hope that addresses these concerns.

Strategy \#2: Contrast Present to Future

The framework:

Current limitations

Future aspirations

The principle: "Value is best understood when it can be compared to something else. Pain cuts twice

when the opposing pleasure is well known."

When you show someone where they are NOW versus where they COULD BE, the gap itself becomes

painful. The bigger and clearer that gap, the more motivated they are to bridge it.

Strategy \#3: Misconceptions

The framework:

Indirect

Use cases

Nuanced

The principle: "If I tell you the limitations about someone LIKE you, you're less likely to get defensive

and shut down, yet you're still likely to be influenced."

Instead of saying "YOU are wrong about this," you say "Many people in your position believe X, but

actually..." This allows them to change their mind without feeling attacked.

Strategy \#4: External Pressures

The framework:

States are abstract

Events are concrete

Unmet expectations

The principle: "Specific events (real or proxied) will have more immediate impact (though their depth

isn't as great)."

Talk about specific, concrete events rather than abstract feelings. "The economy crashed in 2008" is more

powerful than "economic uncertainty." Real events create urgency.

Strategy \#5: Past Struggles

The framework:

Near Past

Mid Past

Disruptions

Rahm Emanuel quote: "Never let a good crisis go to waste."

Reference struggles they've faced recently, in the mid-past, or disruptions in their industry. People

remember their pain vividly and don't want to go back there.

Strategy \#6: The Enemy

The framework:

Blame

Suppression

Revenge

The principle: "It is easier to blame others than it is to take responsibility. Hatred is a stronger short-term

motivator than love."

Give them an enemy to fight against. This could be:

A competitor

"The system"

Old ways of thinking

People who have wronged them

Forces keeping them down

Jason uses this carefully but recognizes its power.

Strategy \#7: Anger

The framework:

Lack of control

Violations

Lies

The principle: "Even though change is the only constant, we continue to delude ourselves into business

as usual and view disruption as an annoyance and not an opportunity."

Tap into what makes them angry - the lies they've been told, the violations of trust, the lack of control

they feel. Anger is motivating.

Strategy \#8: Informed Pessimism

The Cycle:

1\. Uninformed Optimism - "This is going to be great\!"

2\. Informed Pessimism - "Now I know all the ways this can go wrong"

3\. Crisis of Meaning - "Is this even worth it?"

4\. Informed Optimism - "I know the challenges, AND I know how to overcome them"

5\. Crash & Burn - If you don't make it through

The approach: "You now know all the ways in which it can go 'wrong'."

Walk them through the informed pessimism phase in your webinar. Show them everything that could go

wrong, every obstacle, every challenge. Then show them your solution addresses all of it, moving them to

informed optimism.

IMPLEMENTING PAIN IN YOUR WEBINAR STRUCTURE

Where to Use Pain

Jason's guidance: "Most people are too carrot and not enough stick. The stick is more motivating."

Pain can be inserted into:

Pop Quizzes

Fortune Telling hooks

"The Tall Task" setups

Throughout the presentation

Most powerfully during the close

Jason's note: "Pain is most useful during the close. That's usually where people will use it. I try to use it

throughout, but it's most useful during the close."

Calibration: The Balance of Pain

Too much pain = no hope If you make their life too dark and blurry, they won't have any motivation to

do anything ever with you or otherwise.

Not enough pain = no desire to change If there's not enough pain, they won't feel compelled to take

action.

The sweet spot: There's a balance point between too much and not enough. You must calibrate based on:

The price point (higher prices need more pain)

The market sophistication

The severity of the problem

Jason's formula: "The higher the price, the more pain you need to work with. The bigger the ticket you

sell, the more pain there is that you have to work."

PRACTICE THE FLIP: TURNING BENEFITS INTO PAIN

This is one of Jason's most practical exercises. Every time you state a benefit, practice flipping it to the

pain of NOT having that benefit.

Examples from the Webinar:

Benefit: Look younger Pain flip: "So when you look in the mirror you feel more vibrant. So many people

avoid a mirror and they don't look at the person that they should love the most, which is themselves."

Benefit: Time freedom Pain flip: "Having no choice of what you can do with your day, being limited by

somebody else telling you where you can be and when you can be."

Benefit: Lose 15 pounds Pain flip: "Don't be a fat ass anymore" (Jason notes this one doesn't work well) /

Better: "No longer have to look in your wardrobe and see all of the stuff you can no longer wear anymore.

Now you can wear it again."

Jason's Practice Discipline

"Anytime I hear a positive, I attach a negative to it. I always think in terms of both. Every time I put a

gain, what's the pain? I don't always use both, but I think in both: pain-gain."

Flipping Objections Into Positives

Jason also practices the reverse - when someone comes to him with a complaint or objection, he flips it

into a positive:

Objection: "Jason, it would be irresponsible of me to buy this product cause I just had a newborn baby."

Jason's flip: "Sounds to me like you have an inbuilt reminder of what's at stake. So every time you pick

up your little girl and you look her in the eyes, you have a reason to step up. I wish I had the motivation

that you had right now."

Result: "Okay, I'll buy it."

More flip examples:

Objection: "I don't have enough money" Flip: "Good. Now you have to be resourceful because you're

not full of resources. And guess where the biggest breakthroughs come from? The people that are

resourceful."

Objection: "Jason, I'm brand new" Flip: "Good. You haven't been corrupted by the cynical influences

that are out there yet. You're still a babe in the woods, I can do something with you. You're like fresh clay

in my hands."

Objection: "I'm really experienced" Flip: "Excellence. Now you know where the body's buried. Any

position you give me, I can win with."

Jason's attitude: "That's my attitude. Practice the flip."

THE ETHICAL APPROACH: A TALE OF TWO PAINS

The Mischaracterized Interpretation (WRONG)

Some people interpret pain marketing as:

"Find the weakness. Open the wound. Pour the salt in. Sell the treatment."

Jason's response: "That's a good way of looking at it intellectually, like whack him over the head with a

mallet and give him an aspirin. Some people go about it that way, they really do. I've sat in on those

meetings. I don't want to. There's no amount of money in the world that that's okay."

The Correct Approach: Two Pains Theory

The reality: Your prospects are ALWAYS going to have one of two pains:

1\. Short-term pain = The pain of doing something different, trying something new, taking a risk

2\. Long-term pain = The pain of doing nothing while slowly descending into nothing

Jason's perspective: "If it's two pains, I'm gonna give him the better of the two pains."

Jim Rohn's Version

"There's two pains that we all must suffer: the pain of regret or the pain of discipline. And the pain of

discipline weighs ounces, and the pain of regret weighs tons."

The Change Agent Philosophy

The principle: "The pain to stay the same needs to be greater than the pain to change."

If you really want to change people, this is the attitude you must embrace. When it's too painful to stay

the same, people will naturally change.

The Milton Erickson Lesson

Jason learned this from Milton Erickson, "the greatest hypnotist that ever existed" who essentially

invented conversational hypnosis.

Erickson's approach as a change agent: "When it's too painful to stay the same, you will naturally

change."

The Bandler Example:

Richard Bandler (who brought Erickson's work to the world) would go to psychiatric hospitals where

people were claiming "I'm Jesus reincarnated."

Instead of trying to convince them they weren't Jesus, Bandler would come in with two-by-fours and nails

and start measuring them.

Patient: "What are you doing?"

Bandler: "Well, you're Jesus, aren't you?"

Patient: "Yeah..."

Bandler: "Well, Jesus gets crucified, so..."

Patient: "I'm not Jesus anymore."

The principle: The pain to stay Jesus was greater than the desire to change from Jesus.

Jason's Ultimate Reframe

"If I really want to change somebody's behavior, I don't try to show them how my product is a good deal.

I try to show them how it's more expensive NOT to buy my product. Keeping the money will be more

costly than spending it with me."

This is how you get change much easier.

ADVANCED INSIGHTS: THE WHOLESALE FORMULA EXAMPLE

Jason references "The Wholesale Formula" webinar where he masterfully combined multiple pain

strategies. Here's what he showed:

The Traffic Pain

"Driving traffic is the hardest thing to do when it comes to making money online:

It's expensive

It's time consuming

It requires technical skills

Already established businesses can outspend you and outsmart you

So how do you compete? You don't."

The Conversion Pain

"Most don't realize how insanely hard it is to get a complete stranger to buy something from you online,

sight unseen, just because they happen to stumble across what you're selling.

I spent a decade mastering the science of consumer psychology, and I can tell you this: the more I learn,

the less I feel that I know.

You need to know what to say, and more importantly, what NOT to say to get strangers to spend money

with you. And for that to happen, you need trust."

Jason's summary: "All I've done is just show them all of the ways in which they don't have a snowball's

chance in hell of ever making money online. That's the combo."

This combines:

External obstacles (traffic, conversion)

Informed pessimism (showing how hard it is)

The enemy (established competitors)

Fear (of failure, of wasting money)

THE PAIN REMINDER FOR HOOKS

You can inject pain into various hook types:

Pop Quizzes

Use the quiz to reveal painful truths about where they currently stand.

Fortune Telling

Show them the painful future they're heading toward if they don't change.

"The Tall Task"

Frame the challenge in a way that reveals how difficult their current path is, making your solution more

attractive by comparison.

Jason's note: "Most people are too carrot and not enough stick. The stick is more motivating."

MARKET SOPHISTICATION CONSIDERATIONS

ASM5 Context

When Jason created ASM5, they had:

Already sold several million dollars of previous versions

Customers were tired of hearing about it

Competition was using Amazon as a scapegoat: "This is better than Amazon, screw Amazon, do this

instead"

Jason's response: "Their marketing became reactive. So they were polluting my audience. It was pissing

me off, so this is how I addressed it."

He had to address the pain early and directly, acknowledge the fatigue, and reframe the objections

BEFORE people even raised them.

The lesson: As markets become more sophisticated, you need to:

Address fatigue directly

Anticipate deeper objections

Use more nuanced pain strategies

Be more confrontational with compassion

PAIN AND EMPATHY: THE CELEBRITY PROBLEM

Jason's observation: "When celebrities bitch and moan about the struggles, they get no empathy for that.

They get none. And they should, by the way, cause anybody struggling should get empathy. But they don't

because the prejudice is: you're rich and famous and beautiful, how could you have problems?"

The lesson: "So they miss that step in their webinars. The celebrities do. I gotta teach them better."

Application: If you're seen as successful, you must work HARDER to connect with your audience's pain.

You can't skip the empathy step. You must demonstrate that you truly understand their struggles, even if

you've moved past them.

COMMON MISTAKES WITH PAIN

1\. Not Using Enough Pain

"Most people are too carrot and not enough stick."

2\. Skipping Pain Entirely

"So many webinars miss pain or they barely mention it."

3\. Not Making Pain Front and Center

The pain needs to be there from the beginning, not buried in the middle.

4\. Not Calibrating Properly

Using too much (removes all hope) or too little (doesn't motivate change).

5\. Not Practicing the Flip

Only focusing on benefits without connecting them to the pain of NOT having those benefits.

6\. Disconnecting from Your Audience's Reality

"The more successful you get, the further away you're gonna get from your audience. That's where

success becomes a curse."

THE CONFUSION AND CONFLICTING INFORMATION PAIN

Jason specifically calls out this modern pain point:

The health example: "I study health stuff and I swear the stress it creates for me is a net negative. So

like, oatmeal will kill you. Then this other guy: oatmeal is great for you. And this other guy saying you

gotta eat nothing but meat. And this other guy saying you gotta eat vegetables.

What they do is they look at the whole dataset, they find two or three things inside the dataset, and then

they magnify it. Everybody does it.

So I'm like, forget it. I'm just done. I'm just gonna eat clean foods and I don't really care."

The application: "This is big in this market these days cause information is more available - confusion

and conflicting information."

People are overwhelmed with contradictory advice. Addressing this pain point directly makes you more

trustworthy.

FINAL WISDOM: THE KNOW YOUR CUSTOMER BONUS

Jason emphasizes going to the "Know Your Customer" bonus materials to really break down getting

inside the pain in the brain.

His closing instruction: "Make sure it's there front and center. So many webinars miss pain or they

barely mention it. And practice the flip."

KEY TAKEAWAYS FOR IMPLEMENTATION

1\. Lead with pain, not pleasure - People pay to get out of pain faster than they pay to get pleasure

2\. Subtraction before addition - Remove excuses rather than adding features

3\. Use the pain discovery grid - Systematically explore all dimensions of your audience's pain

4\. Master the 8 pain strategies - Have multiple approaches to presenting pain throughout your webinar

5\. Practice the flip constantly - Every benefit should have a corresponding pain point

6\. Calibrate carefully - Balance between too much (hopeless) and too little (unmotivated)

7\. Higher price = more pain needed - The bigger the investment, the more pain you need to address

8\. Use pain most during the close - While it should be throughout, it's most powerful at decision time

9\. Embrace the two pains philosophy - You're giving them the better of two pains: short-term pain of

action vs long-term pain of inaction

10\. Make pain staying the same \> pain to change - When this equation flips, change happens naturally

11\. Stay connected to your audience's reality - Force yourself to continually see through their eyes,

especially as you become more successful

12\. Address confusion and conflicting information - This is a major modern pain point worth calling

out directly

THE ULTIMATE PAIN REFRAME

"I don't try to show them how my product is a good deal. I try to show them how it's more expensive

NOT to buy my product. Keeping the money will be more costly than spending it with me."

This is the master principle that ties everything together. When you can make them see that the cost of

inaction exceeds the cost of your solution, the sale becomes inevitable.

These notes represent Jason Fladlien's complete teaching on pain from his webinar masterclass. Every

principle here has been tested across millions of dollars in webinar sales.

Jason Fladlien - The Tease (Complete Guide)

Theory + Practice + Psychology

NAVIGATION

FOUNDATIONS

Core Philosophy

What is Teasing?

THE 6 MAIN TECHNIQUES

1\. On This Webinar (Blind Bullets)

2\. Questions (Faux Interaction)

3\. Partial Reveal (Most Advanced)

4\. Open Mind (Challenging Assumptions)

5\. My Goal (Direct Statement)

6\. Friction (Creating Tension)

SUPPORTING TACTICS

"It Gets Better"

"Before We Dive In"

Analogies

Giveaways

Transitions

IMPLEMENTATION

How to Blend Techniques

Copy Patterns

Red Flags

Action Steps

FOUNDATIONS

Core Philosophy: Blended, Not Isolated

Key Principle: Good strategies accomplish multiple things simultaneously.

Jason hammers this throughout:

Study techniques in isolation (like in a lab)

But apply them BLENDED in the real world

Don't just use one technique at a time - layer them

The best webinar slides use 2-3 techniques at once

What is Teasing?

Creating curiosity and anticipation without fully revealing what you're going to share.

Purpose:

Keep people engaged

Create open loops you'll close later

Build excitement

Make them feel they need to stay for the answers

Jason's Dating Analogy: "Just like romance should continue throughout the courtship, not just at the

beginning of it, teasing should occur throughout."

Don't reveal everything on the first date. Build anticipation over time.

THE 6 MAIN TECHNIQUES

1\. ON THIS WEBINAR (Blind Bullets)

What It Is:

Give the OUTCOME without revealing the HOW

Use specific numbers to make it believable

Create "On this webinar you'll discover..." bullets

How to Create Them:

1\. Go through your completed webinar

2\. Extract what you taught

3\. Turn each into a blind bullet

4\. Focus on outcomes, hide the methods

Example: LinkedIn Inception

\- "The 3 word sentence you MUST put on your LinkedIn profile 

   (this opens and closes deals for you automatically)"

\- "Why you're not anywhere in the search results for 99.48% 

   of the 4,200,000,000 yearly searches done on LinkedIn"

\- "10 words you should never put on your LinkedIn Profile 

   (chances are at least one is killing deals right now)"

Why This Works:

Ultra-specific numbers (3 words, 99.48%, 4.2 billion) = can't be making it up

Each bullet creates its own curiosity gap

Mix of what TO do and what NOT to do

Parenthetical asides add extra intrigue

You know the outcome, zero clue on the method

Example: QR Control

"More specifically..."

★ We're going to sell a certain type of Mobile Marketing Strategy...

★ That has been - up until now - completely ignored...

★ And the best prospects are hiding in plain sight

Why This Works:

"certain type" = what type?

"completely ignored" = FOMO

"hiding in plain sight" = you're missing something obvious

Combines PARTIAL REVEAL + BLIND BULLETS + OPEN MIND in one slide

THE PSYCHOLOGY BEHIND BLIND BULLETS

It's not just withholding info - it's creating a curiosity gap the brain NEEDS to close.

When you say "the 3-word sentence" instead of "use a call to action," you're:

1\. Creating specificity that proves it's real (exactly 3 words, not "a short phrase")

2\. Making them feel like they're missing something obvious (it's just 3 words\! how do I not know

this?)

3\. Forcing them to stay to close the loop (the brain can't let it go)

The Genius: The more specific the tease, the more real it feels, BUT the less they can figure it out

themselves.

"12 most searched keywords" - they can't Google that. They need YOU.

BLIND BULLET FORMULAS

Pattern 1: The Number + Thing + Outcome

"The 3 word sentence that closes deals automatically"

"The 12 keywords that bring free money"

"The 9 traffic streams available to any video"

Pattern 2: The Contrast (X to do, Y to avoid)

"The 7 types that work well and the 5 types to avoid"

Creates completeness (both sides)

Pattern 3: The Paradox

"Strategies NOT aimed to get clients... but do anyway"

Brain can't reconcile it, must stay to find out

Pattern 4: The "Without" Frame

"Without having to pay a cent"

"Without any expertise beyond using a smartphone"

Removes the expected barrier

Pattern 5: The Ultra-Specific Number

"99.48% of the 4,200,000,000 yearly searches"

Not "most" - exact percentage

Impossible to be fabricating

Pattern 6: The Parenthetical Aside

"The 12 keywords (basically free money)"

"(chances are you have one and it's killing your deals)"

Adds curiosity within the bullet

Pro Tip: Jason writes these LAST after going through the webinar. "If you can't write these, hire

somebody to write them."

2\. QUESTIONS (Faux Interaction)

What It Is:

Frame teases as questions they SHOULD be asking

Makes them think "Oh, I should know this\!"

Addresses insecurities directly

Creates different urgency than bullets

THE PSYCHOLOGY: Why Questions Hit Different

When you list bullet points: YOU feel like he's teaching you.

When you ask questions: YOU feel like you're discovering you don't know something you SHOULD

know.

"Here are the questions you SHOULD be asking" implies: "Oh shit, I wasn't even asking the right

questions."

Bullets = "Here's what I'll teach you" (you're the student)

Questions = "Here's what you should already be wondering" (you're behind)

This creates different urgency:

Bullets = desire to learn

Questions = anxiety about what you're missing

Example: ASM3

"Kid's Play - Here are the questions you should be asking..."

\- "How easy is it to private label physical products?"

\- "What are the best products with the least competition?"

\- "What profit margins should you go for?"

\- "How much capital up front... a lot right?"

\- "Is the opportunity saturated? Did you miss the gold rush period?"

Then reveals: "I have answers... In fact, better answers than you'll find anywhere\!"

Why This Works:

Mixes simple questions with complex ones

Addresses objections AS questions ("Did I miss it?")

Creates escalating curiosity

Final line creates confidence in presenter

Example: Affiliorama

Shows 3-step process with questions under each:

Select Niche:

"Go big or go small?"

"What can you offer they can't get elsewhere?"

"What if you screw this up?"

Free Offer:

"What content do you put into it?"

"This is harder than it seems isn't it?"

Affiliate Offers:

"What to promote, who to promote?"

"How to promote it so people buy?"

Finally:

"How to get people to sign up in a mind state conducive to buying?"

"This seems easy; it's actually hard as hell\!"

Why This Works:

Questions build complexity as you go

Each step seems simple, then questions reveal depth

Final line is pure FRICTION

Prevents "I can just do this myself"

Shows respect for the difficulty

WHEN TO USE QUESTIONS

1\. Addressing misconceptions - "Is it easier to rank videos than web pages?"

2\. Building complexity - Start simple, each answer leads to more complex question

3\. Creating commitment - "Would you make a valid effort...?" (gets them nodding yes)

4\. Preventing "I can do this myself" - Show the hidden complexity through questions

5\. Different emotion than bullets - Anxiety about gaps vs. excitement about learning

3\. PARTIAL REVEAL (Most Advanced)

What It Is:

Show the FRAMEWORK without the DETAILS

Give enough to understand, not enough to implement

Reveal criteria/characteristics, not specifics

Trickiest to pull off but most riveting

THE PSYCHOLOGY: The Tightrope Walk

From Affiliorama:

"A never ending pursuit of finding, proving, and perfecting opportunities that..."

\- Even at 10% effective implementation, they work

\- Has enough barrier to entry to maintain a long term advantage

\- Can be scaled easily without adding considerable more time

\- Are "whole" in themselves, work independent of anything else

\- Low downside, high upside

What you now know: The CRITERIA for good opportunities

What you still need: The ACTUAL opportunities

It's like showing the combination lock without the combination. They understand the mechanism but still

need you.

The Danger Zone:

Too much = they leave and try it themselves

Too little = they don't believe you have anything

Just right = they understand VALUE but need you for EXECUTION

Example: QR Control

"The moving parts..."

\- "We'll tap into a solution to a major problem akin to businesses 

   flushing money down the toilet"

\- "We'll present it to people who instantly 'get it' so there is no 

   'convincing' or 'selling' or 'closing' anyone\!"

\- "We'll offer it in a unique way never seen before. If you think 

   you figured it out, you're missing the most important part..."

Why This Works:

Reveals the STRUCTURE without the mechanism

"businesses flushing money" = huge pain point

"people who instantly get it" = easy sales

Last line prevents them thinking "oh I know this"

They know the APPROACH but not the EXECUTION

Example: ASM5 - Three Slide Sequence

Slide 1: Voice Their Skepticism

"You might be thinking..."

\- Those are the "1 in 10,000" customers

\- They already had assets and advantages

\- That's gross, what's the net?

\- They did something extraordinary mortals can't achieve

\- They already mined all the gold

Slide 2: Challenge Those Thoughts

"You are hopefully also thinking..."

\- Maybe it IS possible with the right approach?

\- Maybe if I do a bit of what they do, I get a bit of what they get?

\- Maybe it's worth being at least open minded

Slide 3: The Ask

"That's all I'm asking - just be open minded..."

"Let me show you the process we have perfected..."

"Then make your own conclusions - cool?"

Why This Works:

Acknowledges their skepticism (respects them)

Voices their cynical inner dialogue

Offers alternative perspective

"Maybe it IS possible" = plants seed of doubt

Final ask: just be open minded (low barrier)

By voicing their cynicism, he defuses it

WHEN TO USE PARTIAL REVEAL

1\. Complex/proprietary methods - Show framework, hide details

2\. Preventing "I can do this myself" - Show criteria that demonstrate depth

3\. Building credibility - Framework proves you have the answer

4\. Addressing objections - Criteria that solve common issues

How to Create:

1\. Identify your core teaching

2\. Extract the framework/components

3\. Show criteria, not details (don't say "use these 12 keywords" - say "the 3 criteria for perfect

keywords")

4\. Create understanding without implementation

The Test: After your partial reveal, they should feel:

"I get what makes this work" (understanding)

"But I don't know how to actually do it" (still need you)

4\. OPEN MIND (Challenging Assumptions)

What It Is:

Challenge what they think they know

Create cognitive dissonance

For biggest claims in saturated markets

Get them to commit to being open minded

Example: ASM4

"ELUSIVE OBVIOUS"

\- Is the following hype?

\- I'm going to show you how to build a MILLION DOLLAR plus 

  business in less than 12 months...

\- And that's not even the best part\!

Why This Works:

Calls out the objection: "Is this hype?"

Makes huge claim

"That's not even the best part" = more to come

Forces them to question their skepticism

Example: ASM4 - The Vehicle

\- Amazon... but don't get it twisted

\- It's not Amazon that's important...

\- It's how we take advantage of it in a way no one 

  (and I mean no one) capitalizes on it\!

Why This Works:

Pattern interrupt: "It's NOT Amazon"

They think they know (selling on Amazon)

He challenges: "don't get it twisted"

"no one capitalizes" = unique mechanism

Example: QR Control

"I will share with you a strategy using BRAND NEW technology..."

"You think you know... but it's not what you think..."

Why This Works:

Direct challenge: "you think you know"

Then negates: "it's not what you think"

Creates cognitive dissonance

Forces them to question assumptions

WHEN TO USE OPEN MIND

1\. Saturated/cynical markets - Make money online, weight loss, real estate

2\. When making big claims - Without addressing skepticism, they tune out

3\. Counterintuitive mechanism - "Model the $3,500 earner, not the $100K one"

4\. At the beginning - Get them to commit to being open minded

The Sequence:

1\. Voice their cynicism (shows you understand)

2\. Acknowledge it seems too good to be true (removes tension)

3\. Ask them to just be open minded (micro-commitment)

4\. Promise they can decide for themselves (removes pressure)

5\. Then present your case

Pro Tip: You're not fighting their resistance - you're acknowledging it, respecting it, then asking

permission to present an alternative view. Once they give that permission, they'll actually listen.

5\. MY GOAL (Direct Statement)

What It Is:

Be upfront about what you're doing

Build trust through transparency

Set clear expectations

Often for advanced audiences or when positioned as leader

Example: Teaser II

"MY PROMISE"

\- "I'm going to give you the easiest path to financial success..."

\- "With the hope that it excites you so much you'll invest 

   in what I have to offer at the end..."

\- "And if that's the case, I have a special deal for early 

   action takers\!"

Why This Works:

Completely transparent

"With the hope" = honest about intent

No surprise pitch

"Early action takers" = urgency

Builds trust through honesty

Example: WP Monster

"IMPORTANT\!"

\- "We're going to be sharing great tips and tricks today\!"

\- "You'll learn something and your time will be well spent"

\- "However, my chief goal IS to sell you something at the end..."

Why This Works:

Brutally honest

No hiding the intent

Actually builds trust through transparency

Removes the "when's the pitch coming" tension

Example: Gender Intelligence - The Transformation

"MY GOAL"

To get from "we've been striving for 15 years with no results" to

"AH-HA\! It all makes sense now"

Why This Works:

Shows the transformation journey

"From/To" structure

Focuses on THEIR outcome not your sale

Builds trust by focusing on their goal

WHEN TO USE MY GOAL

1\. Build credibility early - "I'm going to teach X hoping you'll buy Y"

2\. Overcome skepticism - Cynical audiences appreciate honesty

3\. When positioned as leader - "I'm here to make you money"

4\. Show transformation - "From confusion to clarity"

The Trust Paradox: Being upfront about selling actually INCREASES trust because:

Removes tension about when the pitch comes

Shows you respect their intelligence

Positions pitch as natural outcome of value

"If I give you so much value you want to buy, that's a WIN for both"

6\. FRICTION (Creating Tension)

What It Is:

Show why it's harder than it looks

Create problems that need solutions

"This seems easy; it's actually hard as hell\!"

Push-pull dynamics

Think seduction

Example: Affiliorama - Classic

After showing simple 3-step process:

"Finally: How to get people to sign up in a mind state conducive 

to buying through your affiliate links?"

"This seems easy; it's actually hard as hell\!"

Why This Works:

Shows the process (looks simple)

Reveals: "actually hard as hell"

Prevents "I can do this myself"

Shows respect for difficulty

Creates need for solution

Example: ASM3 - The Cognitive Dissonance

Slide 1: "Important\!"

\- You'll see people doing $20K-$100K+ per month (lots of them)

\- However, here is who you should emulate in your approach

Slide 2: "Raul"  

\- He's doing around $3,500 in net profit per month on his first product

Why This Works:

Shows big earners

Says "DON'T model them"

Model the $3,500 guy instead

Creates massive cognitive dissonance

Burning question: "Why?"

Must stay to resolve tension

This is genius-level FRICTION

Example: Launchpad - Vision Then Obstacle

Shows exciting vision of worldwide distribution, then:

"HOWEVER"

"there is an axiom in business that rings truest on Amazon"

"CASH IS KING"

Why This Works:

Paints exciting vision

Then reveals the obstacle

Creates tension

Implies: "I have a solution to the cash problem"

Push-pull: exciting vision, but here's the challenge

WHEN TO USE FRICTION

1\. Prevent "I can do this myself" - Show hidden complexity

2\. Create urgency - "Limiting timeframe" / "Once in a lifetime"

3\. Show you understand challenges - Builds credibility

4\. Create cognitive dissonance - Forces them to stay for explanation

The Push-Pull Dynamic:

FRICTION is push-pull:

PUSH: Here's the exciting possibility

PULL: But here's why it's hard

PUSH: But I have the solution

PULL: Which requires \[investment/action\]

Why Jason says "Think seduction":

Don't give everything immediately

Create desire (push)

Create uncertainty (pull)

Provide resolution (your offer)

The Formula:

1\. Show the possibility (excitement)

2\. Reveal the complexity (tension)

3\. Position your solution (relief)

Pro Tip: Don't make it TOO hard or they'll give up. Balance: enough friction to create need, not so much

they feel hopeless.

SUPPORTING TACTICS

"It Gets Better" (Escalating Excitement)

Purpose: Maintain interest by continuously escalating the value.

Example: Ramp Engine

"HOW WOULD YOU LIKE TO MAKE $39 PER CUSTOMER?"

"IT GETS BETTER THAN THIS THOUGH ;)"

"IT GETS EVEN BETTER..."

"But let's not get ahead of ourselves\!"

Why This Works:

Prevents satisfaction

Just as they think "okay that's good"

You raise the stakes

Keeps them leaning forward

When to Use:

Throughout webinar (not just intro)

After showing strong results

Before major teaching sections

Leading up to the offer

Pro Tip: Use 2-4 times in a webinar. Don't overuse or it loses impact.

"Before We Dive In" (Easy Open Loops)

Purpose: Control information flow while creating simple open loops.

Example: Gender Intelligence

"BEFORE DIVING IN"

"Let's first clear up some misconceptions..."

"Quick question: why do webinars work so well?"

\- second most powerful medium for education

\- caters to 80/20 of market

\- more you serve, more you can sell

Why This Works:

Controls pace of info

Creates mini open loop

Allows foundational teaching

Shows you're organized

Common Variations:

"Before we dive in..."

"But first..."

"First though..."

"Hold on, before I show you that..."

When to Use:

1\. Need to establish credibility first

2\. Need to set context

3\. Need to clear objections

4\. Want to control pacing (delay gratification = more desire)

The Power of Analogies

Jason's Philosophy: "Will and I sometimes spend HOURS on coming up with just the right analogy, and

the more foreign the concept that we're teaching, the better the analogy should be."

Key Example: The Real Estate Tenant (Crypto Volatility)

Took 2 hours to develop, solved major comprehension problem:

"Imagine you're the landlord of rental property. You bought it for 

$100K. Bank says it's only worth $50K. How much money did you lose?"

Answer: Zero. You didn't sell it.

"You don't care because you have a tenant who pays rent. But unlike 

most tenants who pay monthly, this tenant pays EVERY SINGLE DAY. 

$5, $10, $20 a day."

"The bots are like this. The more uncertain the market, the more 

cash flow they produce."

"Real estate always goes up over time, right? So while you wait 

for that nest egg, you get cash flow."

Why This Works:

Crypto volatility = scary and confusing

Daily rent from tenant = familiar and desirable

Reframes the EMOTION

Volatility goes from "losing money" to "generates daily cash flow"

That's worth 2 hours because if they don't understand it emotionally, they won't buy a $2K-$10K crypto

bot program.

The Safe Cracker Society (SEO):

"Imagine a group guided by a genius, who across 1,000,000+ web 

properties constantly crack the code no matter when it changes."

"If you got enough safe crackers brute forcing it, you're gonna 

open the safe. 0-0-0-1, that didn't work, 0-0-0-2..."

People couldn't understand SEO, but they understand safe crackers trying combinations then sharing the

combo.

The Test of a Good Analogy:

Does it make the scary thing feel safe?

Does it make the complex thing feel simple?

Does it make the foreign thing feel familiar?

Pro Tip: If you spend 30 minutes and people are still confused, you haven't found the right analogy yet.

Keep going. The right analogy removes the emotional barrier to purchase.

Jason's Philosophy on Giveaways

His Stance: Almost never uses them.

"If you can't keep people captivated with your slides and your words, you shouldn't be doing a damn

webinar. If you have to bribe people to the end, there's something wrong. Fix what you're doing instead of

bribing people."

When He DOES Use Giveaways:

Only when something "drops from the heavens":

1\. Truly special and unique

2\. Not just another ebook or video

3\. Something people genuinely want

4\. Scarce and valuable

Example: Zig Ziglar's Personal Copy

For "Zig Secrets of Closing the Sale" webinar:

Took exact replica of Zig's personal copy of "Over the Top"

Reproduced every dog-eared corner, highlight, margin note

Collector's item - truly scarce and valuable

People TEXT to enter (captured phone numbers for follow-up)

Key Quote on Scarcity: "Scarcity is the most powerful motivator that's ever existed for sales."

The Test: Would YOU personally want this giveaway if you weren't offering it? If no, don't use it. Make

better content instead.

Master Transitions

Why They Matter: "Get really good at this. Show something cool and say 'first though, before we get

into that.'"

Effective Transition Phrases:

1\. "More on that in a second, first..."

Jason's favorite rhythm

Controls information flow

2\. "Does it get better? Yeah, it gets better. However..."

Shows escalation then redirects

3\. "Now..."

Clean shift

4\. Avoid "But"

"When you use the word 'but' it invalidates everything before it"

"I got this really good idea, but..." → Now the idea doesn't seem good

Use "First..." "However..." instead

5\. "More on that in a bit..."

Creates anticipation

6\. "We'll talk about that later, first we have to..."

Sets clear expectations

The Pattern:

1\. Show something exciting

2\. Just as they want to dive in, redirect

3\. Promise you'll come back to it

4\. Move to foundational content first

5\. Eventually deliver on the promise

IMPLEMENTATION

How to Blend Techniques

Study in isolation, apply in combination.

The best webinar slides use MULTIPLE techniques simultaneously.

Example: QR Control (Slide 1)

"More specifically..."

★ We're going to sell a certain type of Mobile Marketing Strategy...

★ That has been - up until now - completely ignored...

★ And the best prospects are hiding in plain sight

What's Happening:

PARTIAL REVEAL: "certain type" (what type?)

BLIND BULLETS: outcome without method

OPEN MIND: "completely ignored" (creates FOMO)

FRICTION: "hiding in plain sight" (you're missing something obvious)

That's 4 techniques in one 3-bullet slide.

Example: ASM3 - Raul Sequence

Slide 1:

"Important\!"

\- You'll see people doing $20K-$100K+ per month

\- However, here is who you should emulate

Slide 2:

"Raul - $3,500 net profit per month on first product"

What's Happening:

FRICTION: Creates confusion (why not model big earners?)

PARTIAL REVEAL: Shows results but not method

OPEN MIND: Challenges assumption about who to model

QUESTIONS: Implied "Why him and not them?"

That's a 2-slide sequence using 4 techniques.

The Key: Don't think "I need to use technique \#3 here." Think: "What's the most effective way to

communicate this?" and naturally multiple techniques will emerge.

Copy Patterns & Language Structures

BLIND BULLET PATTERNS:

✓ "The \[number\] \[thing\] that \[specific outcome\]" ✓ "The \[number\] you should do and the \[number\] you

shouldn't"

✓ "How to \[result\] without \[common obstacle\]" ✓ "Why \[counterintuitive statement\] and \[how to fix it\]"

QUESTION PATTERNS:

✓ Build series that escalate curiosity

✓ Mix simple with complex

✓ Address objections as questions

✓ End with "I have the answers"

PARTIAL REVEAL PATTERNS:

✓ Show criteria, not specifics

✓ Reveal framework, hide details

✓ Give understanding without implementation

FRICTION PATTERNS:

✓ "This seems \[easy\]... it's actually \[hard as hell\]"

✓ Vision → Obstacle → Solution

✓ Cognitive dissonance → Must stay for resolution

Key Mindset Shifts

1\. Don't Let Important Things Come Out of Nowhere

"I don't want anything that I teach that's important to come out of nowhere."

Build up to important reveals with:

Questions that create the need

Analogies that create understanding

Teasers that create anticipation

2\. Specificity Creates Believability

Notice the pattern in good bullets:

Specific numbers: "3 word sentence," "12 keywords," "99.48%"

Specific timeframes: "in 2 hours or less," "in 90 days"

Specific outcomes: "$10,000 plus expenses paid"

The more specific, the more real it seems.

3\. Animation is Your Best Friend

"Animation creates that movement."

Why use it:

Reveals info one piece at a time

Prevents reading ahead

Creates visual interest

Helps control pace

Makes static slides dynamic

Without animation:

People see all 7 bullets at once

Start thinking about \#7 while you're explaining \#2

Miss the emotional journey

With animation:

Forces processing one idea at a time

Creates micro-curiosity gaps (what's next?)

Builds momentum

4\. The Webinar as Seduction

Jason uses this analogy intentionally.

Bad dating = Bad webinars:

Come on too strong immediately

Reveal everything in first 5 minutes

Ask for commitment before building attraction

No mystery, no intrigue, no buildup

Good dating = Good webinars:

Create intrigue and mystery

Reveal yourself gradually

Build attraction over time

"Romance should continue throughout the courtship"

The parallel is exact. You're wooing your audience. Building desire. Leading to a natural commitment that

feels right because of everything that came before.

RED FLAGS TO AVOID

❌  Coming Out Like a Blunt Object Don't just state facts - create mystery and anticipation

❌  Bribing People to Stay If you need giveaways to keep engagement, fix the content

❌  Giving Away Too Much in Teasers Should create desire to know more, not satisfy curiosity

❌  Being Too Vague "I'm going to share something amazing" = empty hype. Add specifics.

❌  Stopping After the Intro Tease throughout. Don't just tease at start then deliver boring content.

❌  Using "But" in Transitions Invalidates what came before. Use "First..." "However..." instead.

❌  Not Having Enough Specificity Generic statements aren't believable. Add numbers, timeframes,

outcomes.

ACTION STEPS

1\. Study Real Webinars Watch with new eyes. Identify which teasing techniques they use.

2\. Practice Writing Blind Bullets Go through your content. Turn each teaching into outcome-focused

bullets.

3\. Develop 2-3 Strong Analogies For your most complex concepts. Spend the 2 hours. It's worth it.

4\. Master Transition Phrases Practice redirecting without "but." Control information flow.

5\. Add Specificity Transform vague claims into ultra-specific, believable statements.

6\. Blend Techniques Don't just use one. Layer 2-3 techniques in each slide.

7\. Test Your Teasers Ask: Would this make ME want to know more? Am I giving away too much or

being too vague?

8\. Remember the Core Philosophy Good strategies accomplish multiple things simultaneously. Study in

isolation, apply blended.

9\. Focus on Content Quality Make content so good you don't need bribes to keep people engaged.

10\. Think Seduction Build anticipation. Reveal gradually. Create desire over time.

KEY QUOTES TO REMEMBER

"Good strategies often accomplish multiple things simultaneously."

"The blind bullet is one of the most versatile tools in any marketer's toolkit."

"Scarcity is the most powerful motivator that's ever existed for sales."

"When you use the word 'but' it invalidates everything before it."

"If you can't keep people captivated with your slides and your words, you shouldn't be doing a damn

webinar."

"Teasing is the name of the game in the beginning of your webinar."

"I don't want anything that I teach that's important to come out of nowhere."

"The more foreign the concept that we're teaching, the better the analogy should be."

"Just like romance should continue throughout the courtship, not just at the beginning of it, teasing should

occur throughout."

RESOURCES

Gary Halbert's Newsletter "The Twist" Teaches how to write blind bullet points

garyhalbertnewsletters.com

32 Slide Decks Jason mentions giving access to actual webinar slide decks to see specific applications

QUICK REFERENCE GUIDE

The 6 Main Techniques:

1\. ON THIS WEBINAR - Blind bullets (outcome without method)

2\. QUESTIONS - Faux interaction (what they should be asking)

3\. PARTIAL REVEAL - Framework without details

4\. OPEN MIND - Challenge assumptions

5\. MY GOAL - Direct statement of intent

6\. FRICTION - Show why it's harder than it looks

Supporting Tactics:

"It Gets Better" (escalating excitement)

"Before We Dive In" (easy open loops)

Analogies (making complex simple)

Giveaways (rarely, only if truly special)

Transitions (control the flow)

Remember:

Study in isolation

Apply blended

Tease throughout

Add specificity

Think seduction

END OF DOCUMENT

STEP 4: EXCITE

Complete Notes from Jason Fladlien's Webinar

Every Technique, Example, Pattern & Insight

# OPENING: WHAT IS EXCITE?

Jason opens by explaining that tease and excite are intertwined, but tease is a specific form of excitement.

The excitement he's talking about includes:

•    Promises

•    Benefits

•    Outcomes

•       Hope

There are universals to consider - certain things that are universally applicable in almost any environment when it comes to what's exciting in the human condition. Jason tries to tap into those as much as possible.

# THE FOUNDATIONAL FRAMEWORK

## Feature → Benefit → Outcome

Jason's Precursor - The Core Distinction:

•    FEATURE = What it is

•    BENEFIT = What it does

•       OUTCOME = How it changes your life

### Example 1: The Drill

•    Feature: The drill

•    Benefit: The hole

•       Outcome: The beautiful family portrait that you will hang on your wall

### Example 2: The Webinar

•    Feature: The webinar

•    Benefit: Make more money

•       Outcome: You never have to look at a price tag again because you made so much damn money

"People say Jason, I tell people this all the time, I don't wanna do a damn webinar. I don't wanna do another webinar just like you don't wanna do another webinar. But we have to do the webinar to make the money - that's the benefit. And then we make the money so we don't have to worry about money - that's the outcome."

## Why This Matters - Jason's Consulting Experience

Jason charges $3,125 per hour for consulting (that's $25,000 divided by 8 hours). He does these consults with very smart, important people that you would know.

Nine times out of ten on these consults, the number one thing is:

"That's a feature dude. You gotta make it a benefit. And then you gotta turn it into an outcome."

It's easy to overlook.

### The Formula - Three Ways to Say It

Version 1:

•    Here's what it is

•    Here's what it does

•       Here's how it makes your life better

Version 2:

•    Here's what it is

•    Here's what it does

•       Here's why tomorrow's gonna be better than today

Version 3:

•    What it is

•    What it does

•       How it changes your life

## The Problem Jason Sees

"Too many features. Not enough benefits. And oh, so few outcomes."

"I want my webinars dripping with benefits and outcomes and pain (which is the reverse - it's just a benefit in different clothing)."

# THE 7 EXCITEMENT TECHNIQUES

Jason isolates specific techniques you can identify in swipe files:

•    Exploit

•    Results

•    Bling

•    Easy

•    Aspirational

•    Story

•       Quick

Plus other techniques: Analogy, Before we dive in, It gets better, Tease the mechanism, Giveaway

# \#1 - EXPLOIT

"It shouldn't be possible under ordinary conditions."

Jason's philosophy on exploit:

"This is my Moby Dick. I am looking for him all the time in the wild. I wanna find something that - yeah, my customers shouldn't be able to make money - however, there's a hack, there's an exploit, there's something, there's a glitch in the matrix that would allow ordinary people to make extraordinary income."

And it does happen. It's a Halley's comet (notice the analogies). It doesn't happen very often, but it does happen occasionally.

## What Is An Exploit?

•    Power reversal

•    Taking advantage of something

•       A real legitimate secret (not the bullshit stuff people say is a secret)

### Questions to Ask:

•    What's the exploit?

•    How can we reverse the power?

•    How can we have somebody take advantage of something?

•       How can we have a real legitimate secret?

## Example: Affiliation (Setting Up The Stage)

"Our goals are aligned. If I can provide a way for you to do that better than anyone else in the market, can you see how we both win?"

So this is where Jason sets up the stage:

"Here's all the stuff that would be wonderful if it could happen. It's never happened before. It's unlikely it should be able to happen. But if it could happen, do you see how we both could win? That gets people really excited."

### His Mission Statement Example:

"I want the fact that you just know me to be your completely unfair advantage that you have over every internet marketer out there. The fact that you just know me."

This is the "who not how" concept - what you know gets you in the door, who you know opens up the safe.

### The Implication That Makes It Exciting:

"I could suck and get a better result. I don't have to get better to get a better result. This is what we're looking for. We want to exploit some sort of opportunity. We want our customers to exploit it. We want them to exploit us to get it."

## Real Example 1: Launchpad (Amazon Product Launch Tool)

Here's how Jason positioned Launchpad:

"Using Launchpad is the most powerful thing you can do to rank your products for your most lucrative keywords, and to do so with less effort, less time, and more cost-effective manners, and to do this for more keywords than ever before."

Then the twist - the 'neutral' framing:

"And it doesn't matter whether you use this to burn customers, to interfere with Amazon's goal, to be selfish with it and only care about you and how much cash it stuffs in your pockets... and it doesn't matter if you use this to help customers find truly better products for them and help Amazon gain more market share and increase customer satisfaction and put yourself last, knowing that if you take care of Amazon and the customer then you can get taken care of and be richer than you ever could imagine... Launchpad don't care. Launchpad don't care."

### Then The Responsibility Frame:

"So promise me, promise me, you will always and only use this tool to enhance the customer experience and to increase the value of Amazon itself. The exploit is there - great power, great responsibility. This thing is so damn good, if you wanted to set the world on fire just to watch it burn, you could. So please don't, please don't do that. But you could. You could. It is that powerful."

### Stack and Dimensionalize Benefits:

Jason points out: "Look at how I even set it up - look at all the different ways that I've essentially said 'to make money'. All these turn of phrases, all these different slight variations of the same thing. This is how we stack and dimensionalize benefits. Stack and dimensionalize things."

His self-critique: "I look at this, I should have more outcomes in here. I have too many features and not enough benefits and outcomes. But you know, live and learn, right?"

## Real Example 2: Amazing.com Promo - Why Giveaways Work

Setting the stage:

"Amazon is, before anything else, a search engine. Just like every search engine, they rely on algorithms to determine the rank of every product for every customer search performed on Amazon. And algorithms can be manipulated."

### Calling It Out Explicitly:

"We say it out loud, we just call it - they can be manipulated. What's the implication that something can be manipulated? What's the implication of that? It means you can exploit it for personal gain. And that's exciting to people whether you want to admit it or not."

Jason adds: "I mean, travel enough of the world and you will see how selfishly people will act, especially the more they're backed into a corner. So we can ignore that or we can lean into it. I prefer to lean into it."

### The Reframe:

"So the implication is, if we know how to manipulate the algorithm, then the only question is: is it ethical or not? Not whether or not I can do it."

Jason's advice: "Always look for the opportunities to really cut people in on a secret. And this was a secret back then - you could essentially give away products and rank on Amazon. Amazon was broken as a search engine. And it still is broken, by the way. But it was especially broken back then."

## The "Kick the Giant" Analogy

This is the analogy Jason uses for exploiting Amazon's algorithm:

"If it doesn't move, we go to the next product. If it stirs, we kick it again. But if it awakens, we win. We win big."

### What Doesn't Matter - Letting Them Off The Hook:

Jason explicitly tells them what does LITTLE TO NOTHING to wake the giant:

•    Great product packaging and design

•    A custom, unique, differentiated product

•       Superior quality of products

"So we're letting him off the hook. You can put it in a white envelope. We show them how to do that, by the way - a white box. And then the reason why, by the way, is we're ecological, we don't wanna harm the environment. So we skipped on the packaging - we turned it into a benefit. So V1 of your product, it's ugly, it's non-different, it's just a me-too product. It's not better or worse or packaged different or did anything amazing, but you can launch it very quickly and you can see if it will rank."

# \#2 - RESULTS

"Everywhere you look you see someone winning\!"

## Key Elements

•    Quantity (and quality)

•    Visual

•       Claim validation

## Real Examples from Jason's Presentations

### Amazing.com Promo

Jason shows multiple screenshots of real earnings, rankings, and success metrics. The slides show 6+ different result screens in succession.

Multiple slides showing different sellers with their earnings and results displayed visually.

Shows customer results with specific dollar amounts and timeframes.

### Gender Intelligence (Barbara Annis)

The Hidden Testimonial Story:

Jason tells this story: "Barbara comes to me to work on it. I go to work and find out she has a testimonial that she's never used in her webinars before. It's a testimonial from Tony Blair - aka the former British Prime Minister. Wow, that's kind of a big deal, isn't it? She's not using it."

The testimonial: "In the two decades scores of diversity consultants have appeared on the scene in global businesses and governments. The best of the bunch is Barbara Annis."

Jason's observation: "And she's not using the damn testimonial. There's so many testimonials, so many people don't use that they should be using."

Jason's process: "When I work with clients, I love it because I go through their testimonials and I find them all. I don't do it for myself because, you know, the cobbler's kids have no shoes. But I do it for others. I sit there and spend hours finding every testimonial I can. Now when you got one like this, you don't need too many other ones for corporate clients. I got the damn former British Prime Minister. I mean, that's pretty damn good."

### The Taylor Swift Story

Jason had another client, Rick Barker, who was Taylor Swift's manager back in the days when she was country.

Rick couldn't use her specifically as a testimonial for contractual reasons (or "the dark underworld that controls the global cabal" as Jason jokes), but they had a video of her when she won a country music award, on stage thanking her manager before God.

"If it was up to me I'd say: 'Interesting, I wanna play you a clip, you tell me your thoughts.' Order me God? So when it comes to showing you how to launch your music career, I don't know, what do you think?"

So they had to use his stuff in roundabout ways - they would sneak Taylor Swift into his webinar presentation in other ways.

Jason's point: "These are character assessment testimonials, not results-based testimonials. So those supposedly are fair game."

### RGT (Multiple Result Slides)

The slides show 6+ successive screens of results from RGT, showing different customer success stories visually.

# HOW TO USE FACTS & STATS

## The STAT TECHNIQUE Pattern

•    Wild Claim - Make a bold statement

•    Stat to back it - Provide specific data/research

•       Tie down - Ask rhetorical question or make conclusion

### Example: Product Eclass

Three slides showing this pattern - wild claim about market opportunity, specific statistics to validate it, then a tie-down question that makes the audience process and agree.

# HOW TO USE TESTIMONIALS

## ASM5 Example - Heavy Testimonial Stacking

Jason shows 8 consecutive slides of customer testimonials for ASM5 (Amazing Selling Machine 5). Each slide features different customers with their results, photos, and success metrics.

The pattern: Show testimonial after testimonial after testimonial. Don't just show 2-3. Show 8, 10, 15+ to create the overwhelming sense that "everyone is winning."

One slide featuring the powerful character assessment testimonial from a former head of state, used for instant credibility with corporate audiences.

•    Different backgrounds (non-English speakers, people in debt)
